lesson plan, every email I haven’t answered, every possible thing that could go wrong in the lab, and even when I tell myself, ‘Stop, this is pointless,’ the loop just keeps running in the background It's like a computer program running in the background that I just can't close.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any intrusive thoughts or images that are disturbing or recurrent, in addition to your worries about responsibilities?\n\nPatient: No, I don't have any disturbing or graphic thoughts like that. It's really just my everyday responsibilities—like school, emails, and my family—looping in my head over and over. My mind is just constantly worrying about those normal, daily things, rather than anything traumatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your excessive worry is typically triggered by a specific event or situation, or is it more of a general, free-floating anxiety that's present most of the time?\n\nPatient: While the new system at school definitely started it, it has turned into a constant, free-floating worry that is there all the time. My mind just jumps from school paperwork to our finances, and then to whether I'm being a good husband and dad. It really feels like a background program running that I can't close, even when I'm just trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, difficulty sleeping, or tension in your muscles that accompany your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel physically revved up, like I've had way too much coffee, yet I'm so exhausted that I drag myself through the school day even when I get to bed on time. My shoulders and neck also get incredibly tight from clenching my muscles, which gives me dull headaches and leaves me sitting at the dinner table with my jaw locked.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of your worries, such as procrastinating on school work or avoiding social events with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately, I've started putting off school tasks like grading and emails until the absolute last minute because I'm just so overwhelmed. I've also been turning down invitations from friends and even missing my son's baseball games. I just feel too drained and keyed up to keep up with anything outside of trying to survive the workday.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically makes you want to avoid these situations or tasks, is it the fear of not doing them perfectly or the feeling of being overwhelmed by the task itself?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. I feel so physically and mentally exhausted from being constantly keyed up that just starting a task feels completely overwhelming. At the same time, I’m paralyzed by a nagging fear of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my students and colleagues, which just makes me put things off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your avoidance of tasks and situations is primarily driven by the desire to escape the emotional distress associated with your worries, or is it more about avoiding the tasks themselves due to fear of failure or perfectionism?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. On one hand, I’m so physically and mentally drained from the constant worry that I just don't have the energy to face those situations. But at the core of it, I’m absolutely terrified of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my family and students."

My shoulders and neck also get incredibly tight from clenching my muscles, which gives me dull headaches and leaves me sitting at the dinner table with my jaw locked.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of your worries, such as procrastinating on school work or avoiding social events with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately, I've started putting off school tasks like grading and emails until the absolute last minute because I'm just so overwhelmed. I've also been turning down invitations from friends and even missing my son's baseball games. I just feel too drained and keyed up to keep up with anything outside of trying to survive the workday.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically makes you want to avoid these situations or tasks, is it the fear of not doing them perfectly or the feeling of being overwhelmed by the task itself?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. I feel so physically and mentally exhausted from being constantly keyed up that just starting a task feels completely overwhelming. At the same time, I’m paralyzed by a nagging fear of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my students and colleagues, which just makes me put things off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your avoidance of tasks and situations is primarily driven by the desire to escape the emotional distress associated with your worries, or is it more about avoiding the tasks themselves due to fear of failure or perfectionism?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. On one hand, I’m so physically and mentally drained from the constant worry that I just don't have the energy to face those situations. But at the core of it, I’m absolutely terrified of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my family and students.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you would still feel the need to avoid tasks or situations if you didn't fear making mistakes or not meeting expectations, or is the avoidance more about the emotional toll of worrying about those potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a combination of both, but the fear of making mistakes or letting people down is what triggers the constant, looping worry in my mind. My shoulders and neck also get incredibly tight from clenching my muscles, which gives me dull headaches and leaves me sitting at the dinner table with my jaw locked.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of your worries, such as procrastinating on school work or avoiding social events with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately, I've started putting off school tasks like grading and emails until the absolute last minute because I'm just so overwhelmed. I've also been turning down invitations from friends and even missing my son's baseball games. I just feel too drained and keyed up to keep up with anything outside of trying to survive the workday.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically makes you want to avoid these situations or tasks, is it the fear of not doing them perfectly or the feeling of being overwhelmed by the task itself?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. I feel so physically and mentally exhausted from being constantly keyed up that just starting a task feels completely overwhelming. At the same time, I’m paralyzed by a nagging fear of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my students and colleagues, which just makes me put things off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your avoidance of tasks and situations is primarily driven by the desire to escape the emotional distress associated with your worries, or is it more about avoiding the tasks themselves due to fear of failure or perfectionism?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. On one hand, I’m so physically and mentally drained from the constant worry that I just don't have the energy to face those situations. But at the core of it, I’m absolutely terrified of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my family and students.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you would still feel the need to avoid tasks or situations if you didn't fear making mistakes or not meeting expectations, or is the avoidance more about the emotional toll of worrying about those potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a combination of both, but the fear of making mistakes or letting people down is what triggers the constant, looping worry in my mind. However, the actual avoidance is mostly due to the sheer emotional and physical toll of that worrying—I'm just so exhausted and keyed up that I don't have the energy left to face those tasks or social situations.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of your daily life is affected by this avoidance, such as what percentage of tasks or social interactions you're able to participate in without feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I'd say a huge portion of my life is affected—I probably avoid or miss out on about 60 to 70% of my usual social and family activities, like my son's baseball games, friends' invites, and my robotics club designs, because I'm just too drained. Even at work, I'm constantly putting off grading and emails until the last minute. It feels like almost none of my day is untouched by this overwhelming exhaustion."

But at the core of it, I’m absolutely terrified of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my family and students.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you would still feel the need to avoid tasks or situations if you didn't fear making mistakes or not meeting expectations, or is the avoidance more about the emotional toll of worrying about those potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a combination of both, but the fear of making mistakes or letting people down is what triggers the constant, looping worry in my mind. However, the actual avoidance is mostly due to the sheer emotional and physical toll of that worrying—I'm just so exhausted and keyed up that I don't have the energy left to face those tasks or social situations.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of your daily life is affected by this avoidance, such as what percentage of tasks or social interactions you're able to participate in without feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I'd say a huge portion of my life is affected—I probably avoid or miss out on about 60 to 70% of my usual social and family activities, like my son's baseball games, friends' invites, and my robotics club designs, because I'm just too drained. Even at work, I'm constantly putting off grading and emails until the last minute. It feels like almost none of my day is untouched by this overwhelming exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your exhaustion and physical symptoms, such as feeling 'revved up' and muscle tension, are more prominent than your worries about daily responsibilities, or are they equally bothersome to you?\n\nPatient: They feel equally bothersome because they are constantly feeding into each other. The non-stop mental loop of worrying about school keeps my body tense and 'revved up' all day, which then directly leads to me feeling completely wiped out and exhausted by the evening."
  }
]

My wife and my principal both suggested I come talk to someone because it's starting to take over my life.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe a specific situation where you felt overwhelmed by worries, and how you typically try to cope with these feelings?\n\nPatient: The other night I tried to watch a baseball game to unwind, but my mind immediately started racing with worries about lesson plans, unanswered emails, and whether I’m failing my students and my family. It really feels like a background program running that I can't close, even when I'm just trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, difficulty sleeping, or tension in your muscles that accompany your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel physically revved up, like I've had way too much coffee, yet I'm so exhausted that I drag myself through the school day even when I get to bed on time. My shoulders and neck also get incredibly tight from clenching my muscles, which gives me dull headaches and leaves me sitting at the dinner table with my jaw locked.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of your worries, such as procrastinating on school work or avoiding social events with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately, I've started putting off school tasks like grading and emails until the absolute last minute because I'm just so overwhelmed. I've also been turning down invitations from friends and even missing my son's baseball games. I just feel too drained and keyed up to keep up with anything outside of trying to survive the workday.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically makes you want to avoid these situations or tasks, is it the fear of not doing them perfectly or the feeling of being overwhelmed by the task itself?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. But at the core of it, I’m absolutely terrified of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my family and students.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you would still feel the need to avoid tasks or situations if you didn't fear making mistakes or not meeting expectations, or is the avoidance more about the emotional toll of worrying about those potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a combination of both, but the fear of making mistakes or letting people down is what triggers the constant, looping worry in my mind. However, the actual avoidance is mostly due to the sheer emotional and physical toll of that worrying—I'm just so exhausted and keyed up that I don't have the energy left to face those tasks or social situations.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of your daily life is affected by this avoidance, such as what percentage of tasks or social interactions you're able to participate in without feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I'd say a huge portion of my life is affected—I probably avoid or miss out on about 60 to 70% of my usual social and family activities, like my son's baseball games, friends' invites, and my robotics club designs, because I'm just too drained. Even at work, I'm constantly putting off grading and emails until the last minute. It feels like almost none of my day is untouched by this overwhelming exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your exhaustion and physical symptoms, such as feeling 'revved up' and muscle tension, are more prominent than your worries about daily responsibilities, or are they equally bothersome to you?\n\nPatient: They feel equally bothersome because they are constantly feeding into each other. The non-stop mental loop of worrying about school keeps my body tense and 'revved up' all day, which then directly leads to me feeling completely wiped out and exhausted by the evening.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your worries and physical symptoms impact your daily functioning and relationships, and do you feel like you're able to find any moments of calm or relaxation amidst all this?\n\nPatient: My constant worry and exhaustion have really strained my patience with my wife and son, and I’m missing his games and putting off school grading. True calm is almost impossible to find; even when I try to read or watch TV to wind down, my mind loops right back to work worries while my body stays completely revved up."
  }

My mind is just constantly worrying about those normal, daily things, rather than anything traumatic.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your excessive worry is typically triggered by a specific event or situation, or is it more of a general, free-floating anxiety that's present most of the time?\n\nPatient: While the new system at school definitely started it, it has turned into a constant, free-floating worry that is there all the time. My mind just jumps from school paperwork to our finances, and then to whether I'm being a good husband and dad. It really feels like a background program running that I can't close, even when I'm just trying to relax.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any physical symptoms like restlessness, fatigue, difficulty sleeping, or tension in your muscles that accompany your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes, I constantly feel physically revved up, like I've had way too much coffee, yet I'm so exhausted that I drag myself through the school day even when I get to bed on time. My shoulders and neck also get incredibly tight from clenching my muscles, which gives me dull headaches and leaves me sitting at the dinner table with my jaw locked.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of your worries, such as procrastinating on school work or avoiding social events with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, unfortunately, I've started putting off school tasks like grading and emails until the absolute last minute because I'm just so overwhelmed. I've also been turning down invitations from friends and even missing my son's baseball games. I just feel too drained and keyed up to keep up with anything outside of trying to survive the workday.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically makes you want to avoid these situations or tasks, is it the fear of not doing them perfectly or the feeling of being overwhelmed by the task itself?\n\nPatient: It’s really a combination of both. I feel so physically and mentally exhausted from being constantly keyed up that just starting a task feels completely overwhelming. At the same time, I’m paralyzed by a nagging fear of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my students and colleagues, which just makes me put things off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your avoidance of tasks and situations is primarily driven by the desire to escape the emotional distress associated with your worries, or is it more about avoiding the tasks themselves due to fear of failure or perfectionism?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. On one hand, I’m so physically and mentally drained from the constant worry that I just don't have the energy to face those situations. But at the core of it, I’m absolutely terrified of making a mistake, missing a deadline, or letting down my family and students.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you would still feel the need to avoid tasks or situations if you didn't fear making mistakes or not meeting expectations, or is the avoidance more about the emotional toll of worrying about those potential outcomes?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely a combination of both, but the fear of making mistakes or letting people down is what triggers the constant, looping worry in my mind. However, the actual avoidance is mostly due to the sheer emotional and physical toll of that worrying—I'm just so exhausted and keyed up that I don't have the energy left to face those tasks or social situations.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of your daily life is affected by this avoidance, such as what percentage of tasks or social interactions you're able to participate in without feeling overwhelmed?\n\nPatient: I'd say a huge portion of my life is affected—I probably avoid or miss out on about 60 to 70% of my usual social and family activities, like my son's baseball games, friends' invites, and my robotics club designs, because I'm just too drained. Even at work, I'm constantly putting off grading and emails until the last minute. It feels like almost none of my day is untouched by this overwhelming exhaustion.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your exhaustion and physical symptoms, such as feeling 'revved up' and muscle tension, are more prominent than your worries about daily responsibilities, or are they equally bothersome to you?\n\nPatient: They feel equally bothersome because they are constantly feeding into each other. The non-stop mental loop of worrying about school keeps my body tense and 'revved up' all day, which then directly leads to me feeling completely wiped out and exhausted by the evening.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your worries and physical symptoms impact your daily functioning and relationships, and do you feel like you're able to find any moments of calm or relaxation amidst all this?\n\nPatient: My constant worry and exhaustion have really strained my patience with my wife and son, and I’m missing his games and putting off school grading. True calm is almost impossible to find; even when I try to read or watch TV to wind down, my mind loops right back to work worries while my body stays completely revved up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your worries and physical symptoms are primarily driven by concerns about your performance, responsibilities, and potential mistakes, or are they more related to feelings of fatigue, weakness, and a general inability to cope with daily life?\n\nPatient: It is definitely driven by my intense worries about my responsibilities and the fear of making mistakes, missing deadlines, or letting people down. While I do feel incredibly fatigued and drained, that exhaustion feels like a direct result of my mind constantly racing and my body being so tense, rather than the root cause of my distress."
  }

Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow. Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
